The possibility envisaged in your memorandum of
July 8 of the eventual flight of the Emperor and the
0
responsible Japanese officials from Japan to China is by
no means fantastic. I have continually spoken of such a
poseibility. I do not, however, think that it 1s a
matter which need give us serious conoern.
-1044
As to what should be done to prepare to meet such a
situation, this would seem to be primarily a military
question which the State Department 1s not competent to
determine.
The directives which we have drawn up for the
guidance of our armed forces after occupying the main
1slands of Japan are largely flexible, owing to many
imponderable factors involved. We are therefore leaving
to the military and civil administrations a wide latitude
in order to permit them to use whatever assets they may
find available for the establishment and maintenanoe of
order and security. It is not believed that the prior
flight from Japan of the Imperial Family and the high
Japanese officials, both military and civil, would
necessitate any fundamental alterations in our directives.
We shall not know - even approximately - until we ocoupy
Tokyo, how many Americans will be needed to carry on the
civil administration because, as I pointed out in the
Post-War Planning Committee, it 1s conceivable that the
Japanese officials, both high and low, may refuse all co-
operation. We must therefore be ready for any situation,
whether the Imperial Family and the high officials flee
O
or remain. Even ir they stay, it is not beyond the bounds
S
of possibility that they may refuse all cooperation in
administering the country.
If the Emperor and his High Command elect to tranefer
the seat of government to Manchuria - it 1s hard to
conceive of there being any expectation on the part of
the

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the Japanese of their being able to maintain successfully
a Japanese regime on the continent unless it were based
on Manohuria - our military problem will simply be
extended.
As regards possible political counter measures, it
might be advantageous for the occupying authorities in
Japan to deolare that the Emperor by having abandoned
his homeland had voluntarily abdioated. The question
whether the military government should thereupon set up
another member of the Imperial Family as the Emperor's
successor or should treat the emperor institution as
having been terminated should be decided in the light of
an estimate of the situation after our ocoupation of
Tokyo. It is quite possible that the fact or the
abandonment by the Emperor of his homeland could be
utilized to great effect as a propaganda point to break
down remaining Japanese resistance.
This whole problem would of course be greatly
altered if Soviet Russia should attaok Japan prior to
our occupation of Tokyo.
CIT.E
Joseph C. Grew

Reference underlying despatoh by Mr. Emmerson, of
894.01/2
February 9, and its enclosures regarding the possible
establishment of an international organization of
"Free Japanese". This 1s an elaboration of an idea
previously advanced by Mr. Emmerson and whioh you have
seen but I bel leve you will want to read the despatch
and its enclosures in full.
You will note that Mr. Emmereon stresses that any
2-1445
such organization should not be officially sponsored by
the United States Government nor possess official or
political charaoter. However, it seems to me that it
will inevitably have a political character. For example,
Mr. Emmerson states that "such an organization might
provide a unifying, stabilizing politioal force of
cruolal value to us" immediately following the oooupation
of Japan. Okano's letter includes the statementg "We demand
that JPEL Japanese People's Emancipation League should
participate in the democratio government of Japan in the
future, because JPEL is one or the main foroes whioh
JK/CS
are able to guarantee a stable peace in the Pactfio". It
seems to me that one of the prinoipal points on which
Mr. Emmerson will have to satiefy the Department is
that the establishment of an organization of "Free Japanese"
S
would not inevitably involve us in political entanglements
which would be embarrassing during the reconstruotion of
Japan and essentially incompatible with our demanding
of unconditional surrender by Japan.
The mechanics of establishing an organization of
this nature would obviously be difficult and involve
matters of interest not only to the State Department
but to other agencies of the Government, and would oal
for careful examination 1f Mr. Emmerson's proposal wer
accepted in prinoiple
NF
It seems

determining whether it would be feasible to establish
an organization such as the one Mr. Emmerson has in

exploring the possibility of indoctrinating and using
Japanese prisoners of war as 1ndividuals would be
for Mr. Emmerson to prepare a paper for consideration
by the Interdivisional Area Committee on the Far East
and I recommend that he be authorized to disouss this
procedure wi th Mr. Blakeslee.

Dear Kr. Dooman:
The attached unclassified study and accompanying chart
show the organization, composition, and probable routine of
the Japanese Board of Information. These materials are
based on data available at the War Department, and are cur-
rent as of this date. Monitored enemy broadcasts have been
the chief source of informetion.
Corrections or comment would be welcomed. Of parti-
894.01/8-245
cular interest would be the interrogation of persons possess-
ing recent connection with some part of the Board of Infor-
mation, however remote, who might give specific descriptions
of the correlation of the Japanese conduct of war and con-
duct of propaganda.
I look forwarc to a visit with you upon your return
and feel assured that you must be happy about the great
strides forward which have been made and to which you have
contributed so much.
Very sincerely yours,
CS/SMS
Buce Buuclio

1. Nost striking of the Japamese admimistrative alterations

meroe and Industry and Agrioulture and Forestry have been reeem-

war, 1s to revert to its original nosition as an independemt mim-
1stry. On August 25, the Greater East Asla Ninistry was abeerbed

of the ministries whose status has not been alterel, new bureaus
and srecial committees have been set ur and old ones abelished to
meet the changed conditions under which the government mast oper-
ate.
Within the Forelgn Minlstry, a Central Bureau for Daaleom om
the Termination of the War was set up to "act as an intermediary

only for supnlying food and quarters for the oeempying ferees, but
also will "receive any demands the Allied command will make to the
Jananese authorities concerned, and assume the responsibility fer
carrying them out."

COMMENT
The Preamble is desigaed to demenstrate that the
legal requirements eeneerning the conelusien of this
trusteeahip agreement have been satisfied. It refers te
the relevant provisiens of the Covenant of the League of
Natiens and to the Charter of the United Nations, witheut
specifying the "states direetly concerned" as provided in
Artiole 79 of the Charter.
The first twe clauses cite Articles 75 and 77 of
the Charter whieh previde im general terms for the
establishment and applieatien of the trusteeship system.
The three soneluding cleusee deal in general with
the erueial question of the "states direetly coneerued"
1n the Japanese Nandated Islands. Artiele 79 of the

1melurding the mandatery pewer in the ease of territeries
held under mandate by & Member of the Umited Natiens, and
shall be approved as provided fer in Artieles as and es.
The phrase "states directly coneerned" was of
Ameriean origin, designed to ensure that the Daited states
would have a voise in the megotiatien of the tems ef
trusteeship fer every mandated territery and every terri-
tery detached frem an enemy state as a result of the Seeemd
Werid War. The Umited states eould olaim te be a "state
direetly concerned" in every mandated territery as a
majer belligerent in the First World War, as eme of the
Prineipel Allied and Associated Powers in faver of which
Germany remeuneed all rights and titles im her eversees
possessions, and as a party to conventions with the
Mandatory Powere in respect of the "A" and "B" Mandates,
and with Japan in respect of the Japanese Nandated Islands.
The United States would have an equal slaim te be a "state
direetly coneerned" in any territory detached frem am
enemy state as a result of the Secend World Mar, as ene
of the sigmatorles of the Italian Armistiee and one of the
acc ptors of the Japanese surrender.
The Ameriean proposal coneerning the negotiatiom of

ARTICLE 1
The Territory ef the Pacifie Islands, eemsisting of
the islands fermerly held by Japan wnder mandate in as-
cerdamee with Artiele 22 of the Covemant ef the League
of Mations, is hereby desigmated as a strategie area
and plaeed under the trustesship system establishbed in
the Charter of the United Nations. The Territery ef the
Pacirie Islands is hereinafter referred to as the trust
territory.
COMMENT
The entire Territory of the Paoifio Islamds is
designated as strategio under the provisions of Artiele 82
of the Charter in order to mest the meeurity requirements
of the United States. The importamee of these seeurity
requirements was olearly shown in the last war. It should
be noted, of course, that this agreement does mot sever
any islands under Japanese sovereignty for whieh the
United States may become responsible but relates enly
to the Japanese Mandated Islands. Ir any other islands
are placed under United States administration the type
of trusteeship will be determined at that time.
This artiole, as in similar artioles in other pro-
posed trusteeship agreements now under consideration,
defines the geographioal extent of the trust territory.
The definition of territory in this article is based upon
the mandate, with the intention that the Unlted States,
in taking over the rights of Jupan, should administer
under trusteeship exaotly the same area as Jupan adminis-
tered under mandate. Thus the trust territory, being
based upon the mandate, is limited to thn land masses of
the islands with their territorial wetors und does not
inolude the millions of square mlies of ocean lying betwoon
he islands.(

CONFIDENTIAL HOTE
As indieated in the Confidential Note under Artiele 5,
the Soviet Union, China, and India have asserted, in the
General Assembly discussion of the New Zealand drart agree-
ment for Western Samoa, that an administering authority
canmot under Artiele 84 of the Charter establish bases er
station forees in a trust territory other tham for "loeal
defense and the maintenance of law and order", ereept
by designation of the territory as strategie and in as-
cordance with a special agreement or agreements semeluded
with the Security Counoil under Artiele 43 of the Gharter.
It thus appears likely that these Members will oppose the
United States draft agreement on the same groumd, making
speoial reference to Artiole 82 of the Charter, upon which
artiele 1 of the draft agreement is based. Artiole 82
reads as follows:
"There may be designated, in any trusteeship
agreement, a strategio area or areas whieh may
inolude part or all of the trust territory to
which the agreement applies, without prejudice
to any special agreement or agreements made
under Artiole 43."
There is no indication in Article 82 that the conolusion
of a special sgresment or agreements under Artiole 43 or
the Charter must preoede the designation of a strategio
aree or arees in a trust territory. The phrese "without
prejudice to" is admittedly ambiguous but it cannot properly
be interpreted as meaning that rtiole 82 cannot becoms
affective until the relevant speoial agreements have been
conoluded. The phrese was intended, it 1s belioved, to
make aure thut e Member could not remove a trust territory
from
CONFIDENTLAL

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.97)
frem the jurisdietion of a special agreement cenelmded
under Artiele 43 mrely by designating all or part ef
that territery as strategie. Thus, in ceneluding a
special agreement under Article 43 a Member weuld comsider
the forces and facilities in a strategie area wnder his
administration in the same mammer as in territories wnder
his sovereigaty.
It would seem mecessary, therefore, to resist any
proposal that the desigmation of the territery as strategie

the prinoiple that the Trusteeship Council in strategie areas

economio, social, and educational matters".

ARTICLE 14
The administering authority undertakes to apply in
the trust territory the provisions of any internatiomal
conventions and recommendations whiah may be appropriate
to the partioular ciroumstanoes or the trust territery and
whioh would be oonducive to the aohievement of the basie
objectives of .rticle 6 of this agreement.
COMMNT
The provision seems to be undeniably desirable in the
interests of the inhabitants since it will seeure for the
inhabitants of the territory the benefits of all appropriate
international conventions and recommendations, inoluding
agreements which already exist as well as those which may
be adopted in the future by the United Nations and its
specialized agencies. Comparable artioles, at the suggestion
of the United States, have been included in all of the draft
agreements submitted by the mandatory powers.
This article however, is broader than the comparable
artioles in the Australian, New Zealand, and French draft
agreements. Article 6 of the agreement for New Guinea
limits the application of international agreements and
recommendations to those which "are in the opinion of the
administering authority suited to the needs and conditions
of the territory..." The New Zealand Artiole XIII in the
agreement for Western Samoa is narrower than the United
States' in two respeots: (1) existing conventions and
recommendations are not inoluded in its soope; and (2) the
phrase "in the opinion of the administering authority" 1s
inoluded just as in the Australian draft. The Frenoh
Article 6 limits the application to "international agree-
ments and conventions whioh are at present in force there..."
(1.e. in the territory).
No provision similar to those in this artiole were
included in the mandate granted to Japan.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
ARTICLE 15
The terms of the present agreement shall not be
altered, amended or terminated without the oonsent of the
administering authority.
COMMENT
Artiole 79 of the Charter provides that "the terms of
trusteeship ... including any alteration or amondmont, shall
be agreed upon by the states directly conoerned, inoluding
the mandatory power ..." In as muoh as eaoh mandatory power
is presumably to be appointed as administering authority
under the trusteeship agreement whioh it is submitting for
approval, reference to alteration or amendment of the terms
of trusteeship for a mandated territory in acoordanee with
Article 79 is comparable, in the case of a mandatory power,
to the present provision that as administering authority
the United States must consent to the alteration, amendment
or termination of the terms of trusteeship for the Paoifio
islands.
The provisions of this artiole are substantially
similar to comparable provisions in the agreements submitted
by the United Kingdom, France, Belgium, and New Zealand for
territories which they intend to place under trusteeship.
The only important difference is the inolusion of the word
"terminated" in the United States agreement. While it oan
be contended that "termination" is one form of "alteration
or amendment" for purpose of Article 79, specifio roforence
to termination removes all possible doubt on this point.

Sen. Warren Austin, Chairman

General Assembly
United Nations
Dear Senator Austin:
We have examined with some care the trusteeship
agreement for the Pacific Islands submitted by our
government in the light of rights to be granted to the
native peoples. We are not concerned with the form of
trusteeship, which is a political matter outside our field,
but with the extension to native peoples of those civil
liberties which ch racterize the United States.
We note in the document some assurances of these
liberties, but they appear to us to lack precision in
two respects:
1. There is no assurance of civil government. If
the traditional policy concerning Guam and Samoa 1s fol-
lowed, arbitrary naval rule under a commander from whom
there is no appeal will govern the 1slands. It requires
no argument to demonstrate that such rule, however benevolent,
cannot achieve the objects stated in the draft agreement.
The experience in Guam and Samoa le eloquent enough
testimony to that.
In our judgment civil government should be provided
for the natives with the Navy left in command of inatalla-
tione in the same manner as our naval and air bases in
other counties.
2. No assurance is  1ven of teaching in native
lan uesee. One of :he worst asnecte of our colonial policy
in the Fhilipnines, Fuerto Rico, Guam and Samoa has been
our insistence on the use of a foreign tongue for native
1..struction. surely netive teachers can be treined or
Americens

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Amerioans found who speak the native language. To
1mpose English is wholly out of keeping with sound
educational practice or the natural feelings of pride
all people have in their own tongue.
May we earnesly request consideration of amend-
ments to the draft agreement to include these guarantees?
We are, with great respect,
Sincerely yours,
/s/ LAURENCE DUGGAN
Chairman,
Committee on Civil Rights
in American Colonies
John Foster Dulles

been laid according to the will of the Japanese people, and hereby sanction
and promulgate the amendments of the Imperial Japanese Constitution
effected following the consultation with the Privy Council and the decision
of the Imperial Diet made in accordance with Article 73 of the said Con-
stitution.

The Constitution of Japan
We, the Japanese people, actung through our duly clected representatives
in the Nattonal Diet, determined that we shall secure for ourselves and our
posterity the frunts of peaceful cooperation with all nations and the blessings
of liberty throughout this land, and resolved that never again shall we be
visited with the horrors of war through the action of government, do pro-
claim that sovereign power resides with the people and do firmly establish
this Constitution. Government is a sacred trust of the people, the authority
for which is derived from the people, the powers of which are exercised by
the representatives of the people, and the benefits of which are enjoyed by
the people. This is a universal principle of mankind upon which this
Constitution is founded. We reject and revoke all constitutions, laws,
ordinances, and rescripts in contlet herewith.
We, the Japanese people, desre peace for all time and are deeply
cons ious of the hugh ideals controlling human relationship, and we have
determined to preserve our security and existence, trusting in the justice and
futh of the peace-loving peoples of the world. We desire to occupy an
honored place im an nternational society striving for the preservation of
peace, and the bamshment of tyranny and slavery, oppression and intolerance
for all time from the caith. We recognize that all peoples of the world
have the right to live in peace, free trom fear and want.
We be hese that no maton is to poersble to tself atone, but that laws ot
pobmead morality are omversad: and thad obedhence to sueh laws is ncumbent

CHAPTER I. THE EMPEROR
Article 1. The Emperor shall be the symbol of the State and of the
unity of the people, deriving his position from the will of the people with
whom resides sovereign power.
Article 2. The Imperial 'Throne shall be dynastic and succeeded to
in accordance with the Imperial House Law passed by the Diet.
Artiele 3. The advice and approval of the Cabinet shall be required
for all acts of the Emperor in matters of state, and the Cabinet shall be
responsible therefor.
Article 4. The Emperor shall perform only such acts in matters of
state as are provided for in this Constitution and he shall not have powers
related to government.
The Emperor may delegate the performance of his acts in matters of
tate as may be provided by law.
Artiele 5. When, in accordance with the Imperial House Law, a
Regency is established, the Regent shall perform his acts in matters of state
in the Emperor's name. In this case, paragraph one of the preceding
article will be appheable.
Article 6. The Emperor shall appoint the Prime Minister as desig-
nated by the Diet.
The Emperor shall appoint the Chief Judge of the Supreme Court as
designated by the Cabinet.
Artiele 7. The Emperor, with the adyice and approval of the Cabmet,
hall perform the following acts in matters of state on behalf of the peophe
Promulgation of amendments of the constutution, laws, cabint!
orders and treaties.
Convocation of the Diet.
Dressolution of the House ot Representatives
Pro lamation ot general clectton of members ot the Diet.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.93)
Attestation of instruments of ratification and other diplomatic
documents as provided for by law.
Receiving foreign ambassadors and ministers.
Performance of ceremonial functions.
Article 8. No property can be given to, or reccived by, the Imperial
House, nor cat any gifts be made therefrom, without the authorization of
the liet.
CHAPTER II. RENUNCIATION OF WAR
Article 9. Aspiring sincerely to an international peace based on
justice and order, the Japanese people forever renounce war as a sovereign
right of the nation and the threat or use of force as means of settling
international disputes.
In order to accomplish the aim of the preceding paragraph, land, sea,
and air forces, as well as other war potential, will never be maintained.
The right of belligereney of the state will not be recognized.
CHAPTER III. RIGHTS AND DUTIES OF THE PEOPLE
Artiele 10. The condhtions necessary for being a Japanese national
shal' be determined by law.
Article 11. The people shall not be prevented from enjoy ng any of
the tundamental human rights. 'These fundamental human rights guaranteed
to the people by this Constitution shall be conferred upon the people of
the and toture penerations as eternal and inviolate rights.
Artiele 12. The freedoms and rights guaranteed to the people by this
Cos ntutron shall be maintained by the constant endeavor of the poople.

No privilege shall accompany any award ot honor, decoration or any
distinction, nor shall any such award be valid beyond the lifetime of the
individual who now holds or hereafter may receive it.
Artiele 15. The people have the inalienable right to choose their
publie officials and to dismiss them.
All public officials are servants of the whole community and not of
any group thereof.
Universal adult sutirage is guaranteed with regard to the election of
pubhe officials.
In all elections, s crecy of the ballot shall not be violated. A voter
shall not be answerable, publicly or privately, for the choice he has made.
Article 16. Every person shall have the right of peaceful petition for
the redress of damage, for the removal of public officials, for the enactment,
repeal or amendment of laws, ordinances or regulations and for other
matters; nor shall any person be in any way discriminated against for
sponsoring such a petition.
Article 17. Every person may sue for redress as provided by law from
the State or a public entity, in case he has suffered damage through illegal
aet of any public otficial.
Article 18. No person shall be held in bondage of any kind. In-
volantary servitude, except as punishment for crime, is prohibited.
Article 19. Freedom of thought and conscience shall not be violated.
Article 20. Freedom of religion is guaranteed to all. No religious
organization shall receive any privileges from the State, nor exercise any
political autbonty.
No person shall be compelled to take part in any religious act, cele-
bration, ite on practice.
The State and its organs shall refram trom religious education or any
other religious activity

Freedom of all persons to move to a foreign country and to divest
thenselves of their nationality shall be inviolate.
Article 23. Academic freedom is guaranteed.
Article 24. Marriage shall be based only on the mutual consent of
both sexes and it shall be maintained through mutual coopertion with
the equal rights of husband and wife as a basis.
With regard to choice of spouse, property rights, inheritance, choice
of domicile, divorce and other matters pertaining to marriage and the family,
laws shall be enacted from the standpoint of individual dignity and the
essential equality of the sexes.
Article 25. All people shall have the right to maintain the minimum
standards of wholesome and cultured living.
In all spheres of life, the State shall use its endeavors for the pro-
motion and extension of social welfare and security, and of public health.
Article 26. All people shall have the right to receive an equal educa-
tion correspondent to their ability, as provided by law.
All people shall be obligated to have all boys and girls under their
protection receive ordinary education as provided for by law. Such com-
pulsory education shall be free.
Article 27. All people shall have the right and the obligation to work.
Standards for wages, hours, rest and other working conditions shall be
fixed by law.
Children shall not be exploited.
Article 28. The right of workers to organize and to bargain and act
collectively is guaranteed.
Artiele 29. The right to own or to hold property is inviolable.

CHAPTER IV. THE DIET
Article 41. The Diet shall be the highest organ of state power, and
shall be the sole law-making organ of the State.
Article 42. The Diet shall consist of two Houses, namely the House
of Representatives and the House of Councillors.
Article 43. Both Houses shall consist of elected members, represen-
tative of all the people.
The number of the members of each House shall be fixed by law.
Article 44. The qualifications of members of both Houses and their
electors shall be fixed by law. However, there shall be no discrimination
because of race, creed, sex, social status, family origin, education, property
or income.
Article 45. The term of office of members of the House of Repre-
sentatives shall be four years. However, the term shall be terminated
before the full term is up in case the House of Representatives is dissolved.
Article 46. The term of office of members of the House of Councillors
hall be six years, and election for half the members shall take place every
three years.
Article 47. Electoral districts, method of voting and other matters
pertaining to the method of election of members of both Houses shall be
fixed by law.
Article 48. No person shall be permitted to be a member of both
Houses simultaneously.
Article 49. Members of both Houses shall receive appropriate annual
payment from the national treasury in accordance with law.
Artiele 50. Except in cases provided by law, members of both
Houses shall be exempt from apprehension while the Diet is in session,
and any members apprehended before the opening of the session shall be
freed during the term of the session upon demand of the House.
Artiele 51. Members of both Houses shall not be held liable outside
the House tor speeches, debates or votes cast inside the House.
Article 52. An ondmary sesston ot the Diet shall be onvoked once
prT Ve
Article 53. The Cad m: noary detemmahe to convoke extraondhitary
sestons ot the Diet When a quarter on more ot the totad mermbers ot

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.96)
cither House makes the demand, the Cabinet must determine on such
convocation.
Article 54. When the House of Representatives is dissolved, there
must be a general election of members of the House of Representatives
within forty (40) days from the date of di-solution, and the Diet must be
(onvoked within thirty (30) days from the date of the election.
When the House of Representatives is dissolved, the House of
Councillors is closed at the same time. However, the Cabinet may in time
of national emergency convoke the House of Councillors in emergency
session.
Measures taken at such session as mentioned in the proviso of the
preceding paragraph shall be provisional and shall become null and void
unless agreed to by the House of Representatives within a period of ten
(10) days after the opening of the next session of the Diet.
Article 55. Each House shall judge disputes related to qualifications
of its members. However, in order to deny a seat to any member, it is
necessary to pass a resolution by a majority of two-thirds or more of the
members present.
Article 56. Business cannot be transacted in either House unless
ne-third or more of total membership is present.
All matters shall be decided, in each House, by a majority of those
present, except as elsewhere provided in the Constitution, and in case of a
tie, the presiding oficer shall decide the issue.
Article 57. Dehiberation in each House shall be public. However,
a secret mecting may be held where a majority of two-thirds or more of
those members present passes a resolution therefor.
Each House shall keep a record of proceedings. This record shall be
published and given general circulation, excepting such parts of proweedings
of secret session as may be deemed to require secrecy.

Article 59. A bill becomes a law on passage by both Houses, except
as otherwise provided by the Constitution.
A bill which is passed by the House of Representatives, and upon
which the House of Councillors makes a decision different from that of
the House of Representatives, becomes a law when passed a second time
by the House of Representatives by a majority of two-thirds or more of
the members present.
The provision of the preceding paragraph does not preclude the House
of Representatives from calling for the meeting of a joint committee of
both Houses, provided for by law.
Failure by the House of Councillors to take final action within sixty
(60) days after receipt of a bill passed by the House of Representatives,
time in recess excepted, may be determined by the House of Represen-
tatives to constitute a rejection of the sand bill by the House of Councillors.

CHAPTER V. THE CABINET
Article 65. Executive power shall be vested in the Cabinet.
Article 66. 'The Cabinet shall consist of the Prime Minister, who
shall be its head, and other Ministers of State, as provided for by law.
'The Prime Minister and other Ministers of State must be civilians.
The Cabinet, in the exercise of executive power, shall be collectively
responsible to the Diet.
Article 67. The Prime Minister shall be designated from among the
members of the Diet by a resolution of the Diet. This designation shall
precede all other business.
If the House of Representatives and the House of Councillors disagree
and if no agreement can be reached even through a joint committee of both
Houses, provided for by law, or the House of Councillors fails to make
designation within ten (10) days, exclusive of the period of recess, after the
House of Representatives has made designation, the decision of the House
of Representatives shall be the decision of the Diet.
Article 68. 'The Prime Minister shall appoint the Ministers of State.
However, a majority of their number must be chosen from among the
members of the Diet.
The Prime Minister may remove the Ministers of State as he chooses.
Article 69. If the House of Representatives passes a non-confidence
resohution, or rejects a confidence resolution, the Cabinet shall resign en
tmasse, unles the House ot Representatives is dissolved within ten (10)

bills, reports on general national affairs and foreign relations to the Diet
and exercises control and supervision over various administrative branches.
Article 73. The Cabinet, in addition to other general administrative
functions, shall perform the following functions:
Administer the law faithfully; conduct affairs of state.
Manage foreign affairs.
Conclude treaties. However, it shall obtain prior or, depend-
ing on circumstances, subsequent approval of the Diet.
Administer the civil service, in accordance with standards es-
tablished by law.
Prepare the budget, and present it to the Diet.
Enact cabinet orders in order to execute the provisions of this Con
stitution and of the law. However, it cannot include penal pro-
visions in such cabinet orders unless authorized by such law.
Decide on general amnesty, special amnesty, commutation of pun-
ishment, reprieve, and restoration of rights.
Article 74. All laws and cabinet orders shall be signed by the
competent Minister of State and countersigned by the Prime Minister.
Article 75. The Ministers of State, during their tenure of office,
shall not be subject to legal action without the consent of the Prime
Minister. However, the right to take that action is not impaired hereby.
CHAPTER VI. JUDICIARY
Article 76. The whole judicial power is vested in a Supreme Court
and in such inferior courts as are established by law.
No extraordinary tribunal shall be established, nor shail any organ
or agency of the Executive be given final judicial power.
All judges shall be independent in the exercise of theu cot serence and
shall be bound only by this Constitution and the laws.
Article 77. The Supreme Court is vested with the rule-making power
under which it determines the rules of procedure and of practice, and of
matters relating to attorneys, the nternal disciphine of the courts and the
ohomostaoon of pudhcial atíaurs.

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.92)
The Supreine Court may delegate the power to make rules for inferior
courts to such courts.
Article 78. Judges shall not be removed except by public impeach-
ment unless judicially declared mentally or physically incompetent to
perform official duties. No disciplinary action against judges shall be
adm nistered by any executive organ or agency.
Article 79. The Supreme Court shall consist of a Chief Judge and
such number of judges as may be determined by law; all such judges
excepting the Chief Judge shall be appointed by the Cabinet.
The appointment of the judges of the Supreme Court shail be
reviewed by the people at the first general election of members of the
House of Representatives following their appointment, and shall be reviewed
again at the first general election of members of the House of Represen-
tatives after a lapse of ten (10) years, and in the same manner thereafter.
In cases mentioned in the foregoing paragraph, when the majority of
the voters favors the dismissal of a judge, he shall be dismissed.
Matters pertaining to review shall be preseribed by law.
The judges of the Supreme Court shall be retired upon the attainment
of the age as fixed by law.
All such judges shall receive, at regular stated intervals, adequate
ompensation which shall not be de reased during their terms of oflice.
Article 80. The judges of the inferior courts shall be appointed by
the Cabinet fron a list of persons nominated by the Supreme Court. All
such judges shall hold otfice for a term of ten (10) years with privilege
of reappointment, provided that they shall be retired upon the attainment
of the age as fixed by law.

CHAPTER VII. FINANCE
Article 83. The power to admimister national finances shall be exer-
cised as the Diet shall determine.
Article 84. No new taxes shall be imposed or existing ones modified
except by law or under such conditions as law may prescribe.
Article 85. No money shall be expended, nor shall the State obligate
itself, except as authorized by the Diet.
Article 86. The Cabinet shall prepare and submit to the Diet for
its consideration and decision a budget for each fiscal year.
Article 87. In order to provide for unforeseen deficiencies in the
budget, a reserve fund may be authorized by the Diet to be expended
upon the responsibility of the Cabinet.
The Cabinet must get subsequent approval of the Diet for all pay-
ments thom the reserve fund.
Article 88. All property of the Imperial Household shall belong,to
the State All expenses of the Imperial Household shall be appropriated
by the Diet in the budget.
Artiele 89. No public money or other property shall be expended or
appropriated for the use, benetit or maintenance of any religious nstitution
or association, or for any charitable, educational or benevolent cnterprises
not under the control of publie authority.

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.94)
CHAPTER VIII. LOCAL SELF-GOVERNMENT
Article 92. Regulations concerning organization and operations of
local public entities shall be fixed by law in accordance with the principle
of local autonomy.
Article 93. The local public entities shall establish assemblies as
their deliberative organs, in accordance with law.
The chief executive officers of all local public entities, the members
of their assemblies, and such other local officials as may be determined
by law shall be elected by direct popular vote within their several com-
munities.
Article 94. Local public entities shall have the right to manage their
property, affairs and administration and to enact their own regulations
within law.
Article 95. A special law, applicable only to one local public entity,
cannot be enacted by the Diet without the consent of the majority of the
voters of the local public entity concerneo, obtained in accordance with law.
CHAPTER IX. AMENDMENTS
Article 96. Amendments to this Constitution shall be initiated by the
Diet, through a concurring vote of two-thinds or more of all the members on
cach Honse and shall thereupon be submitted to the people for ratitication,
which shall reqmre the atfirmative vote of a majority of all votes cast

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.94)
Article 98. This Constitution shall be the supreme law of the nation
and no law, ordinance, imperial rescript or other act of government, or part
thereof, contrary to the provisions hereof, shall have legal force or validity.
The treaties concluded by Japan and established laws of nations shall
be faithfully observed.
Article 99. 'The Emperor or the Regent as well as Ministers of State,
members of the Diet, judges, and all other public officials have the obligation
to respect and uphold this Constitution.
CHAPTER XI. SUPPLEMENTARY PROVISIONS
Article 100. This Constitution shall be enforced as from the day
when the period of six months will have elapsed counting from the day
of its promulgation.
The enactment of laws necessary for the enforcement of this Con-
titution, the election of members of the House of Councillors and the
procedure for the convocation of the Diet and other preparatory procedures
necessary for the enforcement of this Constitution may be executed before
the day prescribed in the preceding paragraph.
Article 101. If the House of Councillors is not constituted before
the effective date of this Constitution, the House of Representatives shall
function as the Diet until such time as the House of Councillors shall be
constitute.

of Councllors serving in the first term under this Constitution shall be
three vears Members falling under this category shall be determined in
crondante with law.

Subjeet: Policy of New Ashida Government.
JUN30S

POCATION
The Honorable
The Seoretary of State,
Washington.
Siry
I have the honor to report that Prime Minister-Designate ASHIDA
Hiteshi, at his own request, informally called upon me today in my
894.01/2-2448
capasity as Chief of the Diplomatic Section, General Heedquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, with a view to apprising me
of the policies which he hopes to pursue after having formed a Cabinet.
Dr. Ashida stated that he had today called upon General MacArthur and
subsequently upon Brigadier General Courtney Whitney, Chief of the
Government Section, and was assured both by General MacArthur and by
General Whitney of their support. He stated that his relations with
the Government Section had always been most cordial and that he has
never experienced any difficulties from that quarter.
In discussing the question of forming his Cabinet, Dr. Ashida
stated that he does not anticipate any particular diffioulty in this
regard in view of the support of the so-called left-wing Socialists
who, he said, are wholeheartedly in favor of the new Cabinet. It was
Dr. Ashida's impression that the leaders of the left wing are chiefly
concerned wdith being allocated important posts in the Cabinet. He
said that the present propaganda whioh is being ciroulated by certain
Socialist left-wing elements is merely to "cover up" their rather con-
tradiotory action in having forced the resignation of the Katayama
Cabinet and now agreeing to support a new coalition Cabinet under the
leadership of the Democratic Party.
CS/
As a matter of int erest, there is enclosed copy of a statement
which Dr. Ashida said he read to General MacArthur today. The state
ment purports to contain the chief policies which dll be followed by
A
the new Cabinet. These policies are summarized as follows: (1) to
build up a now Japan worthy of world trust; (2) to enhance the demo-
cratization process and social stability; and (3) to push economic
reconstruction.
Of particular interest is the explanation contained under the
X7
heading of economic reconstruction. It is stated therein that
priority will be given to key industries and "the Government will be
prepared to take over control of such industries in case of necessity".

STATEMENT READ BY DR. ASHIDA HiTOsh1 TO GENERAL MaGARTHUR ON
FEBRUARY 24, 1948
Having been given the mandate to form a new cabinet, by virtue
of the decision taken by the Diet, I avail myself of this oppertunity
to seek Your Exwellency's esteemed and valued views on the matter and
at the same time to submit an outline of what I believe to be most
vital policies of the Government.
In view of the situations confronting us at present, both at home
and abroad, I sincerely believe that there is no alternative for me but
to carry out a firm "middle of the road" policy. To save Japan, I 
fully determined to fight against both axtremes, the rightists and
leftists, and in organising the new cabinet this will be my fundamental
polioy.
I  confident that this will be possible becanse of the support
assured me by the Social Democratic Party, the People's Cooperative
Party and the Democratic Party as well as by the support I m expeet-
ing to obtain from the so-called leftist elements of the Liberal Party.
However, in order to successfully carry out my task, to bring
about a cabinet which will truely reflect the will of the people and
which will effectively combat the various difficulties that beset this
nation, the full support of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers is the foremost and most important requisite, for without such
assurance and help, no one can hope to succeed.
The most vital policies of the new Government are as follows:
1) To build up a New Japan worthy of world trust;
Utmost efforts will be made to faithfully carry out our
responsibilities as enumerated in the Potsdam Declaration.
Every cooperation and aid will be offered toward successful
accomplishment of the Occupation aims.
2) To enhance democratization process and social stability;
Since I am of the belief that Communism and extreme political
views are inimical to the healthy growth of denocracy and also in view
of the fact that Red elements are exerting deteriorate influences smong
Japanese labour circles at present, leading to disruption of reconstruc-
tion efforts, firm steps will be taken to combat them.
Vigorous measures to purify political circles are contemplated
and revision of the Election Law is planned.
The Government will take the lead in efforts to thoroughly
diffuse democratic principles among the people.
3) To push economic reconstruction;
Determined
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.96)
Ench to
Telge's 118,
Fobreary 24, 1948.
CORIDDTIAL
-2-
Determined war against inflation will be lawmched. As eme
means, all-out efforts will be contimwed toward imcreesed predestiem.
Priority will be givem to key industries and the Govermmemt will
be prepared to take over control of such industries in case of
neceesity.
Every encouragement will be given to small and medium-sised manu-
facturers in order to stimulate export trade.
To encourage and promote the working will of the people in order
to assare industrial recovery only rational administrative adjustments
are contemplated.
As to the labour problems, which require tactful handling in view
of the fast that the working class is mow definitely a political power,
I am confident that the new cabinet will be able to secure, in coopere-
tion with the Social Democratic Party, the support of all the labor
organisations of moderate tendency.
Though the above is a very brief and sketehy version of my cen-
victions regarding the course which the new cabinet should take, it
is nevertheless presented to you in all sincerity and soed faith with
determination to carry them through to the best of my abilities in the
event the reins of government are entrusted to me.
Your Excellency, on the brink of my formal acceptance of the heavy
responsibility, I wish to promise to you my utmost efforts and coopere-
tion for the successful oontinuation of your task in Japan.

An cssembly elso hcs the power to regulate itself and determino to
a certcin extent its own membershir. This power in itself is very
important as it could deprive the elootorete for a certain period of
timo of full reprosentation. This powor is exeroised by the Assembly
when it passes on certuin quelificttions of its members and when it
exercises its right to disciplino its mumbors for misconduct even to the
extent of expulsinn. This right of discipline must alweys be exeroised

person being disoiplined hes boen elucted by the poople and the people
will be, et the next eluotion, the finel judge of the momber. (Seotion
IX).
Some of the most far reaohing powers ever to be exeroised by an
assembly in Japan are provided in Artiole loo. It is often necessary
for an assembly during the course of its existence to investigate the

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.98)
affairs of the local entity and during the investigation to
dertain information. The assembly as it may deem neoesmary. or
committee (as will be shewn in a later paper) conduoting the in
gation, has the power to summon persons to give information. Thim
person must appear. If the assembly determines that this persen has
given a false statement, he is subjeot to imprisonment. If, however.
prior to the oonolusion of the investigation. he tells the truth, then
the assembly may not refer the porson to the proourator for prosooution.
The essembly itself has no power to infliot punishment; it can only
rofer the facts to the proourator for whatever aotion he may deoide to
take.
Cne olass of persons is exempt from testifying pefore the
assembly during the course of investigation. This exemption applies
to a porson who is celled upon t• give testimony or prosent reoards
whioh he belioves to be official seorets. Under thi oondition he
oan so declare to the assembly. The assembly must then obtain the
consent of the governnent or publio offioe ancerned before the person
is foreed to give the testimony or produo thelreoords. If within
30 days after being requested, the governmont o publie offioial (1)
dees not deglare the matter in question opntrary to the publio in-
terest if disolosed, or (2) gives its consont, thon the testimony must
be given or the records produoed. (Artile l00)
This power given to essemblies to investigate is extremely im-
portant as it may involve the right of the essembly to question the
aots of thu ohief of the local publio body by demanding reports and
examining his manegement of the affeirs of the looal body, his method
of exeoution of resolutions and the manner of raising revenue and the
expenditure thoreof. (Artioles 98 and 121) I do not have to remark
that sueh a profound and sober condition simply could not have existed
under the old laws and Imperial ordinanoos. This great ohange, how-
ever, is precisoly as it should be.

This does not apply to the management of looal affairs only.
It also applies to national affairs dolegeted to the ohief of the
looal publio body. If the matters cre conoerned with the publie
benefit, a written statement can be sent to the administrative offie

ADMINISTRI.TION, FUNCTIONS AND RESPONSIBILITIES
The officials of departments and seotions, the seoretariat and

Thore is but one tnd only one justification for the existenoe of a

In the representctive form of government, a legislature exists

In tho large modern governments of today bureauoraoies are necessary.

intricate, formal organization, innumerable degrees of speoialization

be callcd by the Chairman or upon written request by a majority of the
committee. After the committec has heard, studied, and debated the
testimony of witnesses and experts as the case nay be on the bill, the
nembers must then prepare an exhaustive report setting forth their
decision and their reasons therefor. The report should be available to
all. These open and closed hearings, because of the overlapping nature
of the public questions, will be held by more than one committee  mhare
called joint hearings. But the rospcnsibilities cannot be divided; each
committee has to assume its own rosponsibilities.
4. The powor or privilege of the subpoena is inhcrent in
representative governnent. On far too many occasions in grave public
natters, cortain people aro noedod to testify and particular documents,
books, or records have to bc produced for they alono possess the secret
parts to what would otherwise be a bad puzzle. The subpoona is gencrally

adequato writton instruments. Penalties should and must be meted out when
such antagonism is encountercd. Severe penalties must be imposed, for
oither oral or written misleading or falso information. In this regard
no oxcuses can be tolcrated for such wrong doing is criminal in nature.
It is an insult to the peoples' representatives. It certainly is a
flaunting of the public justice. The conmittee has this great power today
which it never had before. It should exercise it carefully but fearlessly.
Again, all tostimony should be tabulated, documented and made available
properly "to all concerned".

Subject: Probloms of Local Government and Zand Refor

BEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Honorable
FEB 21 1949
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
1/
I have the honor to forward herewith
memorandum dated
October 13, 1948 reporting a conversation petween Mr. U. 
JOHNSON, American Consul General in Yokohama, and Mr. UoHIYAMA
Iwataro, Governor of Kanagawa Prefecture.
Mr. Uchiyama expressed considerable criticism of what
he termed the "Tokyo bureaucracy" and, by implication, its
counterpart in General Headquarters. Mr. Uchiyama believes that
the basic program of local autonomy in Japanese political
administration is being vitiated by the increasing activities
of national government agencies, of which he says there are
approximately fifty operating in Kanagawa Prefocture entirely
independent of the prefectural administration. As Governor,
Mr. Uchiyama explained, the people and the Occupation hold him
responsible for administration, but his actual authority is
being constantly curtailed.
Mr. Uchiyama was particularly critical of the new police
system under which separate police forces must be operated.
xx
Financially, smaller villages are unable to support independent
police forces, and ir. Uchiyama charged that this system is ill-
advised, inefficient, and uneconomical. At present, Mr. Uchiyama
894.105
pointed out, the Yokohama Police Force is larger than the former
organization for the entire prefecture, but, because of divided
authority, is much less effective.
From other sources this Mission has detected considerable
criticism of the reformed police system with its emphasis on
decentralization. Most criticism of the new system stresses
the added expense of independent police forces for each locality
and the lack of efficiency in a decentrelized system. Some
NE
critios
MAR 9-1949
28
CONFIDENTIAL
GLED
C410
02
Dist

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.95)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 686,
October 22, 1948.
CONFIDEITIAL
-2-
critics of the new system point out thet the prevailing trend
in most other countries is toward nation-wide police forces
enjoying centrulized control. Other critics of the reformed
Japanese police system have expressed considerable alarm over
its alleged inefficiency, believing that the present syetem is
wholly inadequate fron. the public sufety standpoint in the event
of lurge scale lubor or other civil disorders.
Respectrully yours,

down to such operations as the enforeement on the highmays of the
gasoline rationing regulations. Thus, while, as Governor, the peepls
and the Occupation forces hold him responsible in inoreasing meesare,
his actual authority is oonstantly curtailed.
At the same time he felt that Prefectural Government is
unnecessarily increasing in complexity, many of the reforms coming from
Tokyo requiring that a separate section be set up by the Prefecture
to handle that particular project. As an example, he stated that
he is presently being required to establish a separate section,

He was also critical of the extent of the reform of the
police system. He stated thst the requirement that separate police
forces be established by villages down to 5,0o0 population was ill-
advised, inefficient, and uneconomical under the conditions of very
dense population in Japan. He stated that at present the support
of one policeman is approximately ¥l00,000 a year, and that the
smaller villages were unable to afford the cost of an effective
independent force. He stated that under the new system, the
administrative organization of the Yokohama City police force was
larger than the former organization for the entire prefecture of
Kanagawa, and because of the divided authority, much less effective.
He stated that the Agrarian Land Reform program was progressing
satisfactorily in the Prefecture in its broad outlines, and that
XR
the land owners were generally reconciled to the inevitability of
its enforcement. However, it was working very badly in many oases
894.52
as Land Conmittees are declaring forest lands to be "agricultural
land" subject to redistribution with the result that the trees are
894.61
being cut from land not suitable for cultivation and the orosion
and flood problem is thereby being greatly intensified. Even
though such land is in some cases not actually declared to be
agricultural land and placed under cultivation, the threat that it
may be done is sufficient to couse the owners to strip off the
trees in an effort to realize their value prior to the forced sale
of the land. He also stated that there had been a particularly
bad abuse of the law in Kamakura where very high value building
lots made from recovered land on the beach front had been declared
"agricultural land" and subjected to forced sale at very nominal
prices because the owners, unable to build because of the shortage
of materials, had used the land for the cultivation of gardens.
He brunded most of the tenant members of the Land Committees in the
Prefecture as "Communist-like" in their aggressive approach to the
implementation of the law, and stated th=t the "neutral" members
of the Committees were indifferent to the problem and therefore
did not exercise the balence intended in the law. However, he
felt that in spite of the safeguards in the law, natural economic
forces would quickly reassert themselves, and, in one way or another,

TO.
0
These cases are considered significant not only
4TF

bFD

OLI

DAK
cases now under investigation have involved many legisla-

and the later arrest of its Frime Winister. The principal
894.50

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 820
dated December 31, 1948 from the
RESTRICTED
United States Political Adviser
for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject
"Scandals in the Japanese Goverm
SULMARY OF JAPANESE GOVERNMENT SCANDALS
Frepared by Foreign Service Officer Richard B. Finn
I. Principal Cases of Corruption in Government Operations
Arms Disposal Case. This case involves allegations
that the Japanese Government was defrauded in the sale
of scrap metal returned to it by the Allied Powers for
use in the Japanese economy.
In September 1945, General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, returned to the Japa-
nese Government all surrendered military supplies for
the relief and renabilitation of the Japanese economy.
To dispose of the returned supplies, the value of which
has been estimated at about one billion United States
dollars, the government assigned responsibility to its
various ministries.
Disposition of scrap metal was placed under the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry subject to policy
control by the Home Ministry. These two ministries,
upon the advice of Occupation officers, organized the
Arms Disposal Committee (Heiki Shori Iinkai) under the
chairmanship of KOMaTSU Takashi, at that time a director
of the Japan Steel Tube Company. As members of the
Committee, representatives of five large industrial
companies were selected: Japan Iron and Steel Company
(Nihon Seitetsu K. K.); Japan Steel Tube Company
(Nihon Kokan K. K.); Fuso Light Letals Company (Fuso
Kinzoku Kogyo K. K.); Kobe Steel Company (Kobe Seiko
K. K.); and the Furukawa Electric Company (Furukawa
Denki K. K.). Occupation authorities were reported
as stating that they considered business men familiar
with the metal industry to be those best suited to
handle this program.
The Arms Disposal Committee (ADC) commenced
operations in November 1945. It appears to have had
full authority to dispose of all scrap metal, includ-
ing scrapped tanks and planes; this scrap was to be
bought from the Home Ministry and sold at prices fixed
by the Japanese Government. The proceeds were to be
turned over to the Japanese Government. The Committee's
initial task was to inventory the stocks of goods which
it was supposed to handle; Occupation officials,
particularly economic officers of the United States
Eighth Army, indicated their interest in having such
an inventory compiled.
Compilation
RESTRICTED

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
- 2 -
Compilation of an inventory of the metal products
returned to the Japanese Government proved impossible.
Many of the stockpiles were distributed by the Japanese
Government immediately after the surrender because
Japanese authorities apparently thought that only
purely military items, such as ammunition, planes,
and tanks, were required to be surrendered to the
Allied Powers. The stocks of goods which were not
distributed were scattered at many points throughout
Japan. Inventories of these goods were to be prepared
by local police and prefectural officials and to be
forwarded to the Home Minister in Tokyo, who would in
turn submit them to the ADC. Ignorance of this proce-
dure on the part of many officials, aggravated by the
confusion prevailing at the war's end, resulted in
incomplete inventories. The lack of homogeneity among
the scrap products under the Committee's jurisdiction
also posed an accounting problem. In addition there
is reason to believe that illegal raids on these stocks
continued in spite of Occupation orders and Japanese
Government instructions.
The Committee nevertheless proceeded to purchase
and sell scrap in accordance with its authorized proce-
dure. In ay 1946 it made a contract with the Home
Ministry formalizing the arrangements under which it
had been operating. The Committee had been meeting
regularly and had been in close liaison with the
Government and the Occupation, although its liaison
on a local level appears to have been poor. Government
authorities appear, however, to have exercised very
little supervision over the ADC.
The expense of scrapping and transporting metal
products was not offset by the income derived from
relatively low fixed prices. The final accounts of
the Committee as of September 1947 when it ceased
operations showed that it nad received about ¥1,250,000,000;
1ts expenses had amounted to about ¥1,500,000,000. Upon
suspension of its activities it turned over to the
Industrial Reconstruction Corporation (Sangyo Fukko
Kodan) of the Japanese Government about 440,O00 tons
of scrap, the proceeds from which will, it is expected,
more than make up the deficit.
Rumors had been constantly circulating throughout
Japan since the surrender to the effect that large
amounts of the military supplies returned to Japan
by the Allies, including metal products, had been
illegally disposed of and were being hoarded in antici-
pation of rising prices. To investigate these claims,
a committee was establisned in 1947 by the House of
Representatives of the Japanese Diet in accordance with
Article 62 of the new Japanese Constitution; this
committee was designated as the Hoarded Goods Investigation
Committee
RESTRICTED

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
-3-
Committee (Intoku Eusshi Tekihatsu Iinkai) and was headed
by KATo Kanju, a prominent member of the Social Demoeratic
Farty. After a detailed investigation the Kato Committeo
reported at the end of 1947 that there were very large
amounts of goods being kept off the Japanese market by
speculators.
Investigations into the source and whereabouts of
hoarded goods nave continued. In early 1948 the Hoarded
Goods Committee was renamed the Illegal Froperty Trans-
actions Investigation Committee (Futo Zaisan Torihiki
Chosa Iinkai). This committee became known as the Muto
Committee after Kato's resignation. A special subcom-
mittee was specially assigned to investigate the activities
of the Arms Disposal Committee. It is noteworthy that
interest in the "hoarded goods scandal," sup osed to
involve several billion yen worth of oods, has centered
almost exclusively in the scrap netal disposal program,
although these operations were but a smali part of the
total military goods disposal program. Lembers of the
subcommittee charged that the ADC received about 7,O00,000
tons of metal for disposition and had accounted for only
1,300,000 tons; no proof of this charge has been advanced,
however, and the uto Committee has been unable to demon-
strate any conclusive evidence of blackmarket activities
or illegal disposition of goods by the ADC.
Folice and procuratorial authorities of the Japanese
Government have also taken up the case. Chairman Komatsu,
who had resigned from the Committee shortly after his
purge in 1947, was arrested on 0ctober 2, 1948 by the
Tokyo District Frocurator's Office on suspicion of graft
and violation of Occupation orders directing that goods
returned by the Allied Fowers be used for the relief and
rehabilitation of the Japanese economy. The Supreme
Procurator's Office of the Japanese Government through
its local offices commenced investigation of the case in
April of this year and by 0ctober 15, 1948 was reported
to have detained for questioning seventy-two officials
of the former Arms Disposal Committee, all of them con-
nected with the five large industrial companies which had
served as agents for the Committee. An indictment was
returned against Komatsu on the charge that by having
permitted the spending of ¥23,000,000 of the Committee's
funds in entertainment and social activity, he had
violated Imperial Ordinance 3ll requiring compliance
with Cccupation orders and had thereby committed a

Coal Lobby Case
This case centers in charges that portions of huge
loans from the RFE to the mining industry as well as a
"slush fund" collected by certain coal operators were
used to oppose a bill introduced into the Diet for modi-
fied national control of all coal mines in Japan.
In the summer of 1947 the Katayama Cabinet presented
this bill to the Diet in accordance with its platform of
modified socialism. Although the bill became the subject
of intense controversy and was substantially altered as a
result of Diet debates, it was enacted into law in
November 1947.
Coal operators had taken the lead in opposing the
Govern ent's bill. humors were prevalent at the time
the Diet was considering the bili that large funds were
being used to defeat the bill. No large-scale investiga-
tion of these rumors was undertaken, however, either by
IPTIC or by legal authorities until October 1948 when
IFTIC opened public hearings. Frocuratorial authorities
in both Tokyo and Fukuoka entered the case soon there-
after; the case is being investigated under the direct
supervision of the Supreme Procurator's Office rather
than that of the Tokyo District Procurator's Office
because of its nation-wide implications.
Attention has been devoted primarily to finding the
source of funds which may have been used by operators to
lobby against the bill. There appear to have been two

II. Law Enforcement L.easures
Reorganization of the structure of the Japanese
Government under the Occupation has necessitated almost
complete revision of the investigative and law-enforcement
agencies of the government. The cases described above
are providing a stiff test of the effectiveness of the
new system.
Frior to Japan's surrender the Home Linistry was
the key organ in the investigation of alleged illegalities
and apprehension of supposed violators, while the court
system, including the procurators, came under the Ministry
of Justice. As control of the Japanese State became more
centralized, the areas of legal jurisdiction tended to
blend, and in many cases the central government, acting
particularly through the Home Ministry, conducted investi-
gations and prosecutions on a highly arbitrary basis.
Post-war reorganization has separated and decentralized
the various agencies concerned with investigation and
prosecution, and made them autonomous. The task of redefin-
ing areas of jurisdiction and of securing the coordination
necessary for an effective legal system has proved difficult.
It is proposed here to discuss briefly the role of the Diet
investigating committee, the procurators, and the police
in relation to cases involving national government opera-
tions. since very few of these cases have been tried
before courts, the role of the courts will not be discussed.
IrTIC. The Illegal Froperty Transactions Investiga-
tion Committee of the House of Representatives of the
Japanese Diet has played a very important part in the
above cases, both in revealing vital facts and in publi-
cizing them to the Japanese people.
Fower to investigate was not held by the Diet prior
to enactment of the new Constitution. This power, which
is clearly modelled upon that of committees of the United
States Congress in respect to government expenditures,
is a broad one and appears to be designed to buttress the
supremacy of the Diet as the highest organ of state power.
Article 62 of the new Constitution in translation states,
"Each House may conduct investigations in relation to
government, and may demand the presence and testimony of
witnesses, and the production of records."
Under
RESTRICTED

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
- 12 -
Under this authorization the House of hepresentatives
in 1947 established the Hoarded Goods Investigation
Committee under the chairmanship of KaTO Kanju. This
committee made extensive investigations of charges that
large stocks of goods, particularly military goods returned
to the Japanese Government by the Allied Powers, had been
secreted throughout Japan and were being sed for blackmarket
speculation. The committee filed a lengthy report in
December 1947 containing the results of its investigations;
this report described caches of hidden goods that had
been revealed, estimated amounts that were involved in
illegal transactions, and noted the opposition that the
committee had met in its work. In the light of later
developaents it would appear that the report exaggerated
the extent of illegal activity involved; in any case
most of the conclusions drawn appear to nave been lacking
in the substance required for legal action.
In January 1948 this committee, consisting of thirty
members, was reconstituted the Illegal Property Transac-
tions Investigation Committee, still under the chairmanship
of kato. as a result of the resignation of the Katayama
Cabinet the committee was inactive until April 1948;
Muto Unjuro, a Social Democrat generally considered to
be of its left wing, was named chairman to succeed Kato,
who had been named Labor Minister by Frime inister
Ashida. The committee, which for several months was
active primarily in investigating the Nishio case,
established a special subcommittee headed by YOnEI
Terusaburo of the Democratic Liberal Farty, to investigate
the Arms Disposal Committee; TOkUDA Kyuichi, a prominent
member of the Japan Communist Party, has been very active
on this subcommittee. The IFTIC has devoted its attention
in the last six months mainly to the Arms Disposal case,
the Showa Denko case (which it began in May 1948), the
Coal Mine State Control bill case, and several general
investigations of blackmarket activities and corruption
in government circles.
The committee has submitted to the Speaker of the
House of kepresentatives a lengthy report concerning the
Arms Disposal case, the facts of which appear to be
accurate although the charge of gross negligence levelled
against the Arms Disposal Committee seems rather extreme
in the light of the facts described. The committee
deserves the greatest credit for breaking the Showa Denko
case after preliminary police investigations had failed
to do so. The committee's investigations of the other
alleged scandals are as yet in preliminary stages.
IPTIC
RESTRICTED

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.99)
