December 31, 1948.
-13-
IFTIC has its own investigators, who work closely
with the public procurators.
Frocurator System. The present procurator system
was established in November 1947 by a law which made it
a separate and virtually autonomous branch within what
is now the Attorney General's office. The procurators
are organized on three levels parallel to but separate
from the new court system - District Frocurators' Offices
Superior Frocurators' Offices, and the Supreme Procurator's
Office.
The procurators appr hend law violators after appli-
cation to courts, a power shared with the police, and
are solely responsible for prosecuting those suspected
of breaking the law. The procurators are thus faced
with a heavy responsibility since they must arrest and
indict alleged offenders and irepare cases for court.
The fact that the police system was reorganized even
more recently than the procurator system has meant that
the police have been able to give little support to the
procurators in criminal cases which are not of a routine
nature. Post-war conditions in Japan, particularly the
extreme inflation, have resulted in unwillingness of
trained personnel to serve as procurators when more
money and better opportunities are available elsewhere.
As a result, procurator staffs have been undermanned
and have in many cases consisted of poorly trained
officials; there nave been several publicized instances
also where procurators themselves accepted bribes for
desisting from prosecuting cases.
The flood of charges of large-scale corruption in
government activities has imposed a tremendous burden on
procurators, especially those in the Tokyo District
Frocurator's Office, which is responsible for presenting
to the courts criminal cases in Tokyo To. It does not
appear to have been anticipated by anyone connected with
the establishment or initial operation of the present
procurator system that the Tokyo office would be called
on to handle cases as complex as the Showa Denko case.
The procurators have therefore had to improvise procedures
to nandle such cases.
Investigations like the Arms Disposal case and the
Coal Lobby case have, because of their extensive ramifica-
tions, been handled by special agencies within the Tokyo
Superior Procurator's Office in liaison with District
Frocurators' Offices. The Showa Denko case, however, has
been handled largely by the Tokyo District Frocurator's
Office in conjunction with the Tokyo Metropolitan Police
Bureau
RESTRICTED

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
- 14-
Eureau and later the National. hural Police. The Tokyo
police are also active in pursuing the Textile Bureau
investigation; since this case involves primarily black-
market activities rather than financial juggling it is
better suited to police investigation. The Supreme
Frocurator's Office in Tokyo coordinates all these
operations.
The irocurators move very slowly both in investiga-
tions and in preferring charges. For example, the books
of both the Arms Disposal Committee and of the Showa
Denko company were seized many months before the procurators
arrested more than a few minor individuals concerned. In
addition, indictments, which are by law returned against
individuals within ten days of their arrest, are followed
by long periods before trial while the procurators continue
to gather evidence. The new Code of Criminal Procedure,
which comes into effect on January 1, 1949, is expected
to cause great difficulty because of the extensive changes
it makes in the criminal procedure system, even though the
ten-day detention period before an indictment must be
returned is extended to twenty days.
It is of course impossible to judge the effect of
the procurators' work in the absence of trials and court
decisions. They operate in privacy, with relatively
little publicity, and the worth of their evidence and
of their presentation of cases cannot be determined
until a judicial body has had a chance to pass on them.
Police. The Japanese police system has been com-
pletely reorganized under the Occupation. The key to
this revision has been decentralization: the police
system as inaugurated in arch 1948 consists of the
National hural Folice and the Local Autonomous Folice
units. This separation neces itated breaking the former
tightly united police system into separate units all
over Japan with a separate national organization; the
authorized strength of the National hural Police (NRF)
is set at 30,o00 men and that of the municipal police
at 95,000 men. The NhP organization is separately
controlled and administered by the National Public Peace
Loard and the Fref ctural Fublic Feace Committees, while
the municipal police are operated by the local municipal
units; the two organizations are expected to cooperate
closely and in case of national emergency to work
together.
The police have played only a minor role in the
cases under consideration. They have, however, done much
of the original investigating necessary for laying a
foundation for prosecution; their disclosure of blackmarket
activities
RESTRICTED

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820,
December 31, 1948.
-15-
activities opened the way for more sensaticnal discoveries
in both the Showa Denko case and the Textile Bureau
investigations. The nation-wide ramifications of the
Showa Denko case resulted in a call to the NRF in Septem-
ber 1948 for assistance; the NhF since that time has
helped the procurators in collecting evidence and
arresting suspects. The Tokyo Letropolitan Folice have
been especially active in the Textile bureau investigation.
In general tne police are sulordinated in this type of case
to the procurators: the procurators nave directed the
investigations and the authority of tne police to make
the arrests is subject to application to a court through
the Irocurators. In cases of overnment corruption involv-
ing extensive financial transactions, the police serve
largely as agents of tne procurators.
III. Conclusion
The effectiveness of Japan's new legal procedures in
their application to the cases described above cannot yet
be fully evaluated. The ccnclusion is irresistable,
nowever, that Japanese tovernmental and :olitical party
activity is in many important res!ects both corrupt and
extremely inefiicient.
The Illegal Froperty Transacticns Investigating
Committee of the iouse of hepresentatives has rendered
irvelueble service in aisclosine important evidence of
corrupticn and in widely publicizing tnese cases.
IrTIc is not, nowever, reyuired to conform with judicial
standards in its investigaticn of evidence and examination
of witnesses, and it is undeniable that committee members
have been motivated to & considerable degree by desire
to gain publicity and to make public re utations. The
impact of the committee's revelations on the Japanese
public has been profound and this effect by stimulating
interest in cleaner government end politics may well
prove to be the legislative body's most lasting contri-
bution in this regard.
Assisted by Diet investigating committees, the procu-
rators constitute the key crganization for preparing and
prosecuting cases based on charges of corrupt political
and governmental activity. The procurators appear still
to lack the requisite personnel and training to cope fully
with cases involving financial operations of considerable
magnitude on a nation-wide scale. Although charges
of corruption can be made freely in the Diet and in
the press, the procurators must prepare their indict-
ments in conformity with the more exacting requirements
of law, and this has imposed a heavy burden on them.
Indication that cases of corruption often involve many
people
RESTRICTED

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
-16 -
people and many operations in a complex pattern has necess1-
tated deliberate investigation, despite premature and
partial public disclosures. Frocurators have therefore
been forced to move slowly; but in several of the cases
the lapse of time between return of the indictment and
commencement of trial is provin unjustifiably long.
There has been almost no criticism that the procura-
tors or the police have acted unfairly or illegally in
any of these cases.
Several legal points are noteworthy. First is the
problem of immunity of Diet members from arrest while the
Diet is in session; this immunity, subject to exception,
is stipulated in Article 50 of the present Constitution.
Already six Diet members, including Nishio and Ashida,
have been arrested during Diet sessions after petition
has been made through the courts and the Frime Minister
to the presiding officer of the House concerned. The
conflicting public interests of apprehending alleged law
breakers on the one nand and on the other of enabling
the chosen representatives of the people to deliberate
freely upon legislation, nake it a moot question whether
the spirit of the constitutional provision is being
violated. A second significant legel point is raised by
the decision in the Nishio case by the Tokyo District
Court, namely that contributions to an individual, though
of a large amount and later used for political purposes,
need not be registered while political party contributions
must in accordance with law be registered. This ruling
may enable many political leaders now under investigation
to claim that any amounts they may nave received were
contributions to them personally and not bribes or politi-
cal donations. Conversely donors will claim that they
were making contributions to individual political leaders
and were not extending bribes. It seems certain that the
courts will be forced to clarify the distinction between
political contributions and contributions to an individual
for political purposes.
The evidences of corruption disclosed in the cases
now being widely publicized in Japan indicate a wide
extent to illegal and immoral practice in Japanese public
life. Appended as a subenclosure is a list showing the
thirty-four Diet members who have been arrested during
the past two years and indicating their political party
and the charge made against them. It seems reasonable to
say that the standards of public morality and of law
enforcement in Japan have never been of a high order;
the attempt to impose occidental standards has brought
to the surface some of the venal and arbitrary aspects
of Japanese political conduct. It is highly encouraging,
however
RESTRICTED

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
-17-
however, that these aspects are being exposed and that
the public indignation caused by them is so great.
In partial explanation of this situation, the large
need of funds in Japanese politics should be noted. It
is commonly estimated that to run for the Diet costs at
least one million yen, an amount which only the richest
Diet members can afford; currency inflation has aggravated
the seriousness of the problem. The temptation for
politicians to accept contributions is therefore wide-
spread and strong, and too often the situation turns into
one of bribery for political favors. In several of the
instances already described, however, the line between
simple pitical contributions and bribery is indistinct.
The gross inefficiency of many agencies of the Japa-
nese Government as evidenced under post-war conditions is
also a contributory factor. In the chaos prevailing at
the war's end, many vital operations of the government were
virtually suspended. Government assets - both money and
supplies - were taken by people in that period through
fear and ignorance as well as through unlawful intent;
large-scale removals of these assets were possible largely
because of the lack of government supervision and resulted
in the arms disposal scandal and in some of the accusa-
tions being made in the textile scandal. Fapid changes
in the Japanese Government caused frequent shifting of
leading officials and made it difficuit to fix responsi-
bility. The interlocking of tne executive and legislative
branches of the government makes it possible for unscrupulous
politicians to direct to their own advantage the implementa-
tion of laws they have enacted. The operations of the
heconstruction Finance Bank illustrate many of the undesira-
ble aspects of Japanese Government operations today -
shortage of trained personnel, frequent changes in personnel,
lack of field units or of reliable agencies to supervise
use of loans, general control of financial activity by
the Finance vinistry and consequent involvement in political
activity. The very psychology of an inflationary period
when uncertainty in values makes for irresponsibility and
unaccustomed illicit operations in the effort at self-
protection, has played its part.
The effect of Japanese financial corruption upon the
Japanese and the Jr ited States taxpayer has been noted
in the press. Tne squandering of large loans by Japanese
financial insitutions has meant an added imposition on
the already overburdened Japanese taxpayer and has no doubt
greatly increased his indignation at these scandals.
wasting
RESTHICTED

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 820
December 31, 1948.
-18-
wasting of meager Japanese assets also means that American
aid to Japan is prevented from making its most effective
contribution and in addition raises doubts as to the
ability of the Japanese Government to administer the
nation efficiently and honestly.
The defects of Japan's political system are being
brought to light, however, and the effectiveness of its
new legal system tested by the investigations and legal
actions being undertaken. It is hoped that this experience
will strengthen the desire of the Japanese people to
reform their government and will ultimately result in
better government.

Sir
1 have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch no. 820
of December 31, 1948, reporting on scandals in the Japanese Govern-
11
ment and to enTose in that connection an English translation of
the judgment of the Supreme Court of Japan of June 1, 1949, in the
21
se of NISHio Suehiro. Als enclosed is a mimeographed copy of
the judgment in Japanese.
As stated in the despatch wxder reference, Nishio was tried
before the Tokyo listrict Ccurt on charges of perjury before a
Diet committ ee and of violation of a Cabinet Ordinance requiring
the registration of contributions to political parties. Nishio
was acquitted by the Di strict Court and this sentence was upheld
upon appeal to the Tokyo High Court. The Government appealed the
case to the Suprene Court which affirmed the sentence.
The Supreme Court held (1) that so long as Nishio understood
the donat ion to be one to him personally and not to the party,
there was no violat jon of the Cabinet Ordinance, even if the donors
considered the gift to be to the party, and (2) that the original
court's finding that donations made by a group of contractors to
Nishio was a cont ribution to Nishio personally rather than to the
Social Democ ratic Party of which he was a leading member, was a
finding of fact properly to te made by the lower court and one
that was not cont rary to the evidence submitted. The crux of
Nishio's defense was that, since the contractors' contribution
had been made with the express understanding that it would not be
used to assist the leftists within the Social Democratic Party, the
894.
JUL 2 9.1949
FILED
contribution
894
RLSTRICTED
ACTION COPY
RETURN TO DC/R FILES WITHIN 14 DAYS, WITH A NOTATION OP ACTION TAKEN

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 413,
Juno 24, 1949.
-2-
contribution was not a contribution to the party within the meaning
of the applicable Cabinet Ordinarce. The Court upheld this con-
tention. It was al so held that the perjury charge was not justiciable
by the judiciary since no cherge had, as required by law, been pre-
ferred by the Diet committ ee before which the alleged perjury took
place.
The judgaent was notable for a strong and cogent dissent by
Judges MANO airl IWAMAT'SU who on the basis of broad political reason-
ing considered that the lower court had not given due weight to
evicence and logical deductions tending to show that the contribution
was in reality made to the party rather than to Nishio personally.
The dissent argued that legally a gift subject to a condition was
still a valid gift, that in fact there was considerable evidence here
to show that the gift was actually made to the party and not to the
individual. and that Nishio'a evidence did not rebut the presunption
that this was a donation to the party.
Although t he Supreme Court's decision was confined to the facts
of this case, it is anticipated that the judgment will have consider
able bearing on the trials now taking place of Nishio, former Prime
finist er ASHIDA, and others in connection with the Showa Denko case.
The legal test applied by the Supreme Court appears, by emphasizing
the understanding of the recipient and by stressing minor conditions,
to establish a raiher relaxed standard for determining whether a con-
tribution is made to a political party or to an individual. The
conclusion is inescapable that the dissenting judges, by applying a
common-sense standard to political contributions, have proposed a
more realistic criterion.
Respectfully yours,
Cloyce K. Huston

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to

obligation to submit the report prescribed by the
Cabinet Ordinance exists only in cases where financial
aid is rendered to a political party, in other words
only in cases where a political party has a right to
use, derive benefit from, or dispose of such financial
assistance. It is very evident, in the light of the
speeific provision of the Ordinance stating "to the
political party" and from the spirit of the legislaticn,
especialiy Article 5 of the Imperial Ordinance
frenibiting formation of certain organizations and
making public the structure cf such organizations, that
sases are exempted where such right merely belongs to
an individual member of a political party. Therefore,
tne finding of the originai judgment concerning this
point is prcper, and the views insisted upon in point
5 of the (appellant's) arguments and the arguments
based on such views are unacceptable. Therefore, even
assuming, for instance, that the accused falls under
the term, "leader", pre iribed in the Cabinet Ordinance,
viclation of the otliga ion to submit a report required
by the Ordinance require that he understood in his
subjective recognition that the financial assistance
was made to the political party.
However, the finding of the original judgment
concerning the subjective recognition of the accused
rejected, as stated before, the arguments of the
procurater, and accepted as true the accused's plea
and depositions at the criginal trial concerning
his understanding of the receipt of the donation in
question, as is shown by the conclusions indicated in
the original judgment. That the original judgment has
not from the evidence found facts which would consti-
tute a crime is clear from the finding of the original
court, especially the fact that the original judgment
specifically quoted as evidence the accused's statement
at the criginal trial concerning this point. It is
of course within the discretion of the original court
to decide whether or not such a statement by the
accused is to be accepted. Therefore, the arguments
of the appellant in this respect are nothing but
criticism of the discretion of the original court, and
cannot be accepted.
As long as the accused in his subjective recogniti...,
considers the donation as made to an individual but not
to the party, there is no violation of the Cabinet
Ordinance, even supposing that the donors had meant the
donation to the party. Therefore, it is clear that each
of the

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No.. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatcn No.
413, June 24, 1949.
-4
of the remaining arguments of the appellant does not
in any way affect the original decision, and cannot
be accepted.
Hewever, invesligation mads under authority vested
in paragraph 2, Article 434, old Code of Criminal Pro-
cedure reveals that the Law Governing Oath and
Testimony of a Witness at the Diet (Law No. 225 of 1947),
in the light of circunstances of the enactment thereof,
was enactea to prescribe procedures within the Diet
to meet requirenents of investigations by each house
of the Diet concerning naticnal administration.
Because such special provisions as the text of Article
8 and a provise thereof concerning indictment of
perjury in the Diet were laid down, it is proper to
Interpret that internal affairs within the Diet should
be aispesed of by the Diet within its own self-govern-
ing power, and that the charge provided for in the.
said artiele is essential to a public action regarding
the ofiense. Since it is evident, however, that no
charge of perjury was made ty the House of Representa-
tives or the committee indicated in the decision, a
publie action cn this charge is improper. The original
court therefore violated the law by hearing the public
action, carrying out a substantial trial, and acquitting
the ace used. The appeal to the Supreme Court on this
point is veascnable, and he original decision must be
reversed.
Acecluingly the decision is given in accordance
vith the provisions f Articles 447, 455, and paragraph
6 of Articie 364 . he cld Cede of Criminal Procedure
corcerning the above part, and in accordance with
Article 44o uf the suid code concerning the remaining
part.

I have the honor to report on the structure, functions, and
!TNES
operations of the Japanese Ministry of International Trade and
Industry as organized pursuant to the provisions of the Ministry
XR
of International Trade and Industry Establishment Law (Law no. Io
of 1949, promulgated on May 24, 1949), five copies of which are
enclosed.
894.002
The new organization, which went into effect on May 25, 1949,
eliminates the Board of Trade (Boeki-cho) by combining that agoney
with the old Ninistry of Commerce and Industry. It may be assmed
that the reform is intended to streamline Japanese axport controls
XR
and to promote export industries pursuant to the provisions of the
Nine Point Economic Stabilization Program tranmitted to the Jap-
be remembered, in part required the Japanese Government to "im-
prove the operation of foreign trade controls," while point 7 re-
XR
quired the Government to "improve the low effectiveness of the
presant allocation and rationing system, particularly to the end
of maximizing exports."
Chief Cabinet Secretary MASUDA Kanaahichi stated, whon the
894.60
bill for establishment of the Ministry was under discussion, that
the new organization would shift the Governmont's administrative
efforts from domestic to export production and bring all agengie
FILED
interested in foreign trade under centralised gontrol, and that it
could be expected to aid in the rationalisation of export indus-
tries. Additionally it may be noted that the Board of Trade had
come to typify inefficient bureaucracy in the minds of both Jap
FAP
anese and foreign merchants, and it may be inferred that the
Cabinet, from political motives as well as from its desire to
further economic recovery, hopes to accomplish much by the liqui-
dation of an unpopular agency. Because of t he rather cumbersome
organization set up under the new law, however, it is doubtful
whether the change will result in improvements upon the procedures
for which the Board of Trade was so severely oriticised.

Ferson.nel Retrenchment

(Law for the Pixed mter f Personnel in the Administrative Organi-
zations) passd ty te Fifth ational Diet, personnel in the new
Mini stry, incl li yturnal Agencies, is to be reduced from 26,499
to 21,259,  rol at on of approximately 20 per cent. of the 5240

TO
8/A - Mr. Jessup
DATE: August 23, 1949
FROM
SUBJECT: Ryukyus
U.S. administration of Ryukyu has been and continues to
be so badly guided by ambiguous policy pronouncements, so
inadequately supported by higher commands, and so ineptly
managed by Military Government itself that the Ryukyuane are
becoming increasingly articulate in their desire to return
to Japanese rule.
The task of overcoming this popular mood and of maintain-
1ng a friendly and loyal civil population will always be
extremely costly and administratively diffioult, hence the
first reoommendation 1s to:
(1) Review the NSC policy decision to retain the
Ryukyus, by exploring the possibilities of
securing our strategic 1nterests there while
returning "sovereignty" to Japan.
8
6
If this 1s not feasible on military gounds, then the basio
recommendation 1s to:
(2) Encourage Ryukyuane to link their economic and
social developmenta w1th Japan's, end revise
policy statemente accordingly.
But in either case, it will be necessary to carry out
2349
1mmediate reforms in the Military Government establiahment,
as follows:
(3)
Request the Department of the Army to attach
the rank of Major General to the Office of
executive head of Ryukyu Military Government,
and to allow the Department of State some
voice in the selection and indoctrination of
the incumbent.
(4)
Approach Army directly to bring about immediate
reforme in conditions concerning native govern-
ment, land, security of person, economics and
finance.
And,
SECRET

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.96)
SECRET
-2-
And,
(5)
Utilize the opportunity afforded by examina-
tion of GARIOA and EROA budgets to improve
Military Government's personnel, planning and
operations.
It 1s planned to act on (1) at a later date. Mean-
while, a "Draft Directive from the Joint Chiefs of Staff
to Commander-in-Chief, Far East, for Military Government
of the Ryukyu Ialands" has been sent 1nformally to and
discussed with the Undersecretary of the Army. This
draft incorporetes the substance of recommendation (2)
through (5). Some of the suggested reforms will probably
be undertaken without further action by State. Those not
acted upon will be pressed by State when the Army Under-
secretary returns from his projected trip to Japan.
